We must now re-check our H\"older condition \eqref{Global:low:first_hold_cond} with our new condition \eqref{Global:low:p_1_gronwall_condnow} and \eqref{Global:low:term1.2:q_1_lower_bound} (notice that this condition is strictly stronger then our initial \(p_1\) condition \eqref{Global:low:special_term_1.1_p_1_lower}),
    \begin{align*}
        \tfrac{1}{2} > \tfrac{7}{4} - \beta + 2\left(\tfrac{1}{2} -s\right)\vee 0.
    \end{align*}
    For \(s\geq 1/2\) we verify that,
    \begin{align*}
        \tfrac{1}{2} > \tfrac{7}{4} - \beta \iff \beta > \tfrac{5}{4}
    \end{align*}
    which is satisfied in our range of $\beta$. For $s < 1/2$,
    \begin{align*}
        \tfrac{1}{2} > \tfrac{7}{4} - \beta + 2\left(\tfrac{1}{2} - s\right) \iff s> \tfrac{9}{8} - \tfrac{\beta}{2}
    \end{align*}
    wich is satisfied by assumption. Setting,
    \begin{align*}
        \alpha &\coloneqq \max\left(\theta_1^* + 1, \theta_4 + 1, \theta_1+\theta_2 + 1,2\theta_4+1\right)
    \end{align*}
    and returning to \eqref{Global:freq_cut:cubic_pregron} we obtain,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|w\right\|_{H^{\beta/2}}^2 &\leq  C(s,\beta,u_0,v_0)\max_{i\in \{1,2\}}\left(C_i^{\text{Term 1}}, C_i^{\text{Term 2}}\right)\left\|w\right\|_{H^{\beta/2}}^\alpha.
    \end{align*}
    for some $C(s,\beta,u_0,v_0)>0$. Given we have conditioned that each term in the maximum be strictly less than 2, we know that $\alpha<2$. Therefore, integrating this inquality yields the following growth on $w(t)$,
    \begin{align}
        \left\|w(t)\right\|_{H^{\beta/2}} \leq \left(\left\|w_0\right\|_{H^{\beta/2}}^{1-\alpha} + C(s,\beta,u_0,v_0)\max_{i\in \{1,2\}}\left(C_i^{\text{Term 1}}, C_i^{\text{Term 2}}\right)(1-\alpha)t \right)^{\frac{2}{2-\alpha}}.\label{Global:frequency_cut:w_growth_poly}
    \end{align}
    Using this, can iterate in steps $T_{w \, \text{existence}}$ to yield a solution $w(t)$ on $[0,T]$. Combining this with our solution $v(t)$, we yield our desired solution $u(t) = v(t) + w(t)$ to \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} on $[0,T]$. Furthermore, using \eqref{Global:frequency_cut:w_growth_poly} and \eqref{Global:frequency_cut:v_T_bound} we obtain the growth bound for $t\in [0,T]$,
    \begin{equation}
          \left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^s} \leq 2T^{-\frac{1}{2}}\vee \left(\left\|w_0\right\|_{H^{\beta/2}}^{1-\alpha} + C(k,s,\beta,u_0,v_0)\max_{i\in \{1,2\}}\left(C_i^{\text{Term 1}}, C_i^{\text{Term 2}}\right)(1-\alpha)t \right)^{\frac{2}{2-\alpha}}\label{Reg_2:growth_bound}.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{Regime_2:restriction_rem2}
    Within the stated regime, this argument fails for $k>3$. If we extend our $\beta$ to $\beta \leq 2$ however, the numerology allows for $k<7$. This mirrors the $s>1/2$ global well-posedness for the quintic BBM obtained in \cite{kim2026} using the same method. Regime 1 improves this result to $s\geq 1/3$ so there is no need to use the above regime for the higher $\beta$ values.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{Regime_3:proposition_result}
     Let $\mu \in \{-1,1\}$, $d=1$, $k\geq 3$ be an odd integer and $\frac{2k-2}{k+1} \leq \beta \leq 2$. Then \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} is globally well-posed in $H^s$ for $s>1/2$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
For $u_0\in H^s$, we again define the corresponding mollified data $u_0^\varepsilon = u_0*\eta_\varepsilon$ and its local evolution $u^\varepsilon$. Using Lemma \ref{global:critical:general:lem_energy} we get that,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|^2_{H^s_\beta }&= -\frac{1}{k}\left\langle \langle \partial_x \rangle_\beta^{2s -\beta} u^\varepsilon, \partial_x\left((u^\varepsilon)^k\right) \right\rangle_{L^2}\\
        &=  -\frac{1}{k}\left\langle \langle \partial_x \rangle_\beta^{s} u^\varepsilon, \langle \partial_x\rangle^{s-\beta}_\beta \partial_x\left((u^\varepsilon)^k\right) \right\rangle_{L^2}.
    \end{align*}
    Estimating and then using Lemma \ref{Lem:multiplier_lemma} to change into our standard $H^s$ multipliers we obtain
    \begin{align}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt} \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|^2_{H_\beta ^s} &\lesssim \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta}\left\|\langle \partial_x\rangle^{s-\beta}_\beta \partial_x \left((u^\varepsilon)^k\right)\right\|_{L^2}\notag\\
        &\lesssim \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta} \left\| \langle \partial_x \rangle^{s-\beta+1} (u^\varepsilon)^k\right\|_{L^2} = \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta} \left\|(u^\varepsilon)^k\right\|_{H^{s-\beta+1}}.\label{Regime3:initial_bound}
    \end{align}
     We now inspect the norm of the nonlinear term. If $\beta>1$, we apply the Sobolev inequality \footnote{Here, by the Sobolev inequality we mean that the embedding $F^s_{\frac{2}{2\beta-1},2}(\mathbb{T})\hookrightarrow F^{s-\beta+1}_{2,2}(\mathbb{T}) = H^{s-\beta+1}(\mathbb{T})$ holds. See \cite[Theorem 3.5.5 (ii)]{schmeisser1987} for this result.} and then our fractional Leibniz rule for the $F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{T})$ spaces, Proposition \ref{Frac_F_rule} to obtain
    \begin{align}
        \left\|(u^\varepsilon)^k\right\|_{H^{s-\beta +1}} \lesssim \left\|(u^\varepsilon)^k\right\|_{F^{s}_{\frac{2}{2\beta-1},2}(\mathbb{T})} &\lesssim \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{F^s_{2,2}}\left\|(u^\varepsilon)^{k-1}\right\|_{h^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}}\notag\\
        &\sim  \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}\left\|(u^\varepsilon)^{k-1}\right\|_{L^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}}\label{Regime3:2nd_split}.
    \end{align}
    In second inequality we note that we have required that $s > \tfrac{2\beta-1}{2} -1$, but
    \begin{align*}
        \tfrac{2\beta-1}{2} -1 < \tfrac{1}{2} \iff \beta <2.
    \end{align*}
    Therefore, $s>\tfrac{2\beta-1}{2}-1$ is satisfied by our $s>\tfrac{1}{2}$ assumption. In the last line, \eqref{Regime3:2nd_split} we have used the fact that  $\frac{1}{\beta-1} >1$ given $\beta <2$, meaning the Hardy space $h^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}(\mathbb{T})$ (defined in Definition \ref{Def:F_space}) is equivalent in norm to $L^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}(\mathbb{T})$.

    If $\beta=1$, we simply apply the standard fractional Leibniz rule like so,
    \begin{align}
        \left\|(u^\varepsilon)^k\right\|_{H^s} \lesssim \left\|(u^\varepsilon)\right\|_{H^s}\left\|(u)^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^\infty}^{k-1}.\label{Regime_3:beta1_case}
    \end{align}
    
    We now distinguish two cases:

    \underline{Case 1:} If $\beta>1$ and $\frac{k-1}{\beta-1}\leq k+1$, then by the finite measure of $\mathbb{T}$, we have that
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|(u^\varepsilon)^k\right\|_{H^{s-\beta+1}} \lesssim \left\|u^{\varepsilon}\right\|_{H^s}\left\|u^{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{k+1}}^{k-1}.
    \end{align*}
    Using Lemma \ref{Global:critical:general:Lk1_control} on the $L^{k+1}$ norm and returning to \eqref{Regime3:initial_bound} we obtain
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta}^2 \lesssim \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^{k-1}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}^2.
    \end{align*}
    using Lemma \ref{Lem:multiplier_lemma} to exchange the $H^s$ norm for a $H^s_\beta$ norm, using Gr\"onwall's inequality and finally taking $\varepsilon \to 0$ with \eqref{global:Hamiltonian_limit} and \eqref{Global:solution_limit_Hs} we get that,
    \begin{align}
        \left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^s}\leq \left\|u_0\right\|_{H^s}\exp\left(C(k,s,\beta,u_0)\mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^{k-1}t\right)\label{Regime_3:Plain_exp}
    \end{align}
    where $C(k,s,\beta,u_0)>0$.

    \underline{Case 2:} Now assume that $\frac{k-1}{\beta-1}>k+1$. If $\beta>1$ we interpolate between $L^{k+1}$ and $L^{\infty}$.
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^{\frac{k-1}{\beta-1}}}\lesssim \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^{k+1}}^{\frac{k+1}{k-1}(\beta-1)}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^\infty}^{1-\frac{k+1}{k-1}(\beta-1)}.
    \end{align*}
    Returing this to \eqref{Regime3:initial_bound} with \eqref{Regime3:2nd_split} as well as using Lemma \ref{Global:critical:general:Lk1_control} to deal with the $L^{k+1}$ norm we yield and Lemma \ref{Lem:multiplier_lemma} to exchange the $H^s$ norm for a $H^s_\beta$ norm
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta}^2 \lesssim \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^{(k+1)(\beta-1)}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta}^2\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^\infty}^{k-1-(k+1)(\beta-1)}.
    \end{align*}
    The above inequality also holds for $\beta=1$ as we simply return \eqref{Regime_3:beta1_case} to \eqref{Regime3:initial_bound} and apply Lemma \ref{Lem:multiplier_lemma}. Setting $\alpha = k-1-(k+1)(\beta-1)$ and $\gamma = (k+1)(\beta)$ we note that $\alpha \leq 2$ by our assumption that $\beta \geq \frac{2k-2}{k+1}$. Now, we invoke the Brezis Gallou\"et inequality, (Lemma \ref{lem:Brezis-Gallouet}) to obtain
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|^2_{H^s_\beta}&\lesssim \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u^\varepsilon_0)^{\gamma}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta}^2\left(1+ \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^{1/2}} \sqrt{\log\left(\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}+1\right)}\right)^{\alpha}\\
        &\lesssim \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u^\varepsilon_0)^{\gamma}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta}^2\left(1+ \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^{\beta/2}} \sqrt{\log\left(\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}+1\right)}\right)^{\alpha}.
    \end{align*}
    Noting that $\left\|u\right\|_{H^{\beta/2}}$ is conserved by Lemma \ref{Global:lemma:Conservervation}, we get that,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|^2_{H^s_\beta} \lesssim \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u^\varepsilon_0)^{\gamma}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta}^2\left(1+ \mathscr{E}_{\beta,1}(u^\varepsilon_0) \sqrt{\log\left(\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}+1\right)}\right)^{\alpha}.
    \end{align*}
    Now simplifying we obtain,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta} \lesssim \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u^\varepsilon_0)^{\gamma}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s_\beta}\left(1+ \mathscr{E}_{\beta,1}(u^\varepsilon_0) \sqrt{\log\left(\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}+1\right)}\right)^{\alpha}.
    \end{align*}
    Letting $y(t) = \left\|u^\varepsilon(t)\right\|_{H^s_\beta}$ and change variables to $y(t)= e^{z^\varepsilon(t)}$ to get,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d}{dt}z^\varepsilon(t) \leq C(k,s,\beta) \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u^\varepsilon_0)^{\gamma}\mathscr{E}_{\beta,1}(u^\varepsilon_0)^{\alpha}\left(z^\varepsilon(t) + 1\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
    \end{align*}
    for some $C(k,s,\beta)>0$. In order to integrate this inequality we require that $\alpha/2 \leq 1$ which is satisfied by the fact that $\alpha \leq 2$. Moreover, if  $\alpha < 2$  we obtain a polynomial growth bound on $z^\varepsilon(t)$,
    \begin{align*}
        z^\varepsilon(t) \leq \left((z(0)+1)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}} + C(k,s,\beta,u_0) \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u_0)^{\gamma}\mathscr{E}_{\beta,1}(u_0)^{\alpha} (1-\alpha)t\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha/2}}.
    \end{align*}
    Recalling the definition of $z^\varepsilon(t)$, applying Lemma \ref{Lem:multiplier_lemma} to switch to a $H^s$ norm as well as using \eqref{global:energy_limit}, \eqref{global:Hamiltonian_limit} and \eqref{Global:solution_limit_Hs} to take $\varepsilon\to 0$. we yield the following growth bound
    \begin{align}
        \left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^s}&\leq \exp\left(\left(\left(\log \left\|u_0\right\|_{H^s_\beta}+1\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+ C(k,s,\beta,u_0) \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^{\gamma}\mathscr{E}_{\beta,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^{\alpha} (1-\tfrac{\alpha}{2})t\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha/2}}\right).\label{Regime_3_growth_bound}
    \end{align}
    If $\alpha=2$, we obtain an exponential growth bound on $z^\varepsilon(t)$,
    \begin{align*}
        z^\varepsilon(t) \leq (z(0)+1)\exp\left(C(k,s,\beta) \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^{\gamma}\mathscr{E}_{\beta,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^2t\right).
    \end{align*}
    Again recalling the definition of $z^\varepsilon(t)$, using Lemma \ref{Lem:multiplier_lemma}, \eqref{global:energy_limit}, \eqref{global:Hamiltonian_limit} and \eqref{global:energy_limit} we get that
    \begin{align}
         \left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^s} \leq \exp\left(\left(\log \left\|u_0\right\|_{H^s_\beta} +1\right)\exp\left(C(k,s,\beta) \mathscr{H}_{k,1,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^{\gamma}\mathscr{E}_{\beta,1}(u_0^\varepsilon)^2t\right)\right).\label{Regime_3_growth_bound2}
    \end{align}
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{Regime_3:tech_remark}
    Simpler techniques for proving this regime were ineffective. One possible avenue is introducing a case distinction on the sign of $s-\beta+1$ at \eqref{Regime3:2nd_split} instead of using the Sobolev inequality into $F^s_{\frac{2}{2\beta-1}}(\mathbb{T})$. This distinction would involve using the fractional Leibniz rule when $s-\beta+1$ is positive and duality when it is not. This introduces several restrictions including $k\leq 7$, making the approach not viable. As such, we instead embed directly into a space of regularity $s$, introducing the quasi-Banach complications.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Combine Propositions \ref{Regime_1_THM}, \ref{Regime_2:prop_result} and \ref{Regime_3:proposition_result}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Let $u_0\in H^s(\mathbb{T}^2)$ and define the it's mollification $u_0^\varepsilon = u_0 *\eta_\varepsilon$ and it's corresponding local evolution under \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq}, $u_\varepsilon$. Using Lemma \ref{global:critical:general:lem_energy} and the self adjointness of $\langle D_x\rangle$ we get that,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}^2 &= -\frac{1}{3}\left\langle \langle D_{x}\rangle^{2s-2} u^\varepsilon, \left(\partial_x + \partial_y\right)\left((u^\varepsilon)^3\right)\right\rangle_{L^2}\\
         &=-\frac{1}{3}\left\langle \langle D_{x}\rangle^{2s-1} u^\varepsilon, \langle D_{x}\rangle^{-1}\left(\partial_x + \partial_y\right) \left((u^\varepsilon)^3\right)\right\rangle_{L^2}.
    \end{align*}
    Now, given that $H^s(\mathbb{T}^2)\hookrightarrow W^{2s-1,2/s}(\mathbb{T}^2)$ we use H\"older's inequality to obtain,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}^2 &\lesssim \left\|\langle D_{x}\rangle^{2s-1}u^\varepsilon\right\|_{{L^{2/s}}}\big\|\langle D_{x}\rangle^{-1}\big(\partial_x + \partial_y\big) \left((u^\varepsilon)^3\right)\big\|_{L^{\frac{2}{2-s}}}.
    \end{align*}
    Using our embedding as well as the $L^p$ boundedness of the operator $\langle D_x\rangle^{-1}(\partial_x + \partial_y)$ we get that,
    \begin{align}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|^2_{H^s}\lesssim \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}\big\|(u^\varepsilon)^3\big\|_{L^{\frac{2}{2-s}}}\lesssim \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}\big\|u^\varepsilon\big\|^3_{L^{\frac{6}{2-s}}}.\label{Dimension_2:global:pre_interp}
    \end{align}
    Now, interpolating the $L^{\frac{6}{2-s}}(\mathbb{T}^2)$ norm we obtain,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{L^{\frac{6}{2-s}}} \lesssim \left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|^{1/3}_{H^s}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|^{2/3}_{L^{4}}.
    \end{align*}
    Returning this to \eqref{Dimension_2:global:pre_interp} and using Lemma \ref{Global:critical:general:Lk1_control}  we yield,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}^2 \leq  C(s)\mathscr{H}_{3,1,2}(u^\varepsilon_0)^2\left\|u^\varepsilon\right\|_{H^s}^2
    \end{align*}
    for some $C(s)>0$. Now, after an application of Gr\"onwall's inequality and using \eqref{global:Hamiltonian_limit} and \eqref{Global:solution_limit_Hs} to take $\varepsilon\to 0$, we obtain the following growth bound,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^s} \leq \left\|u_0\right\|_{H^s}\exp\left( C(s)\mathscr{H}_{3,1,2}(u_0)^2 t\right)
    \end{align*}
    thereby proving global well-posedness for $1/2\leq s <2$. The peristence of regularity of the solution map yields the global result for $s \geq 2$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{Dim_two_restriction_remark}
    It is not clear to the author how to extend this result to a lower range of $\beta$. If we were to adapt our derivative splitting energy method from Regime 1 to the two dimensional setting, we would demand the following upper and lower bounds on our shared derivative, $\sigma$,
    \begin{align*}
        s-\beta + 2 - \tfrac{2}{k+1} <\sigma < s-1 + \tfrac{2}{k+1}.
    \end{align*}
    For there to be room for such a $\sigma$ to exist, we would require that,
    \begin{align*}
        \beta > 3- \tfrac{4}{k+1}.
    \end{align*}
    For any $k\geq 3$, this forces $\beta >2$, leaving us outside the bounds of the model. Using a high-low wavelength decomposition like the one done by Kim and Kwak in \cite{kim2026} for the cubic in dimension 1 was also ineffective, after completing the calculation for general $k$ one is left with the restriction that $k<3$, excluding the cubic equation.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{Intersection:Local:multilinear_lem}
    For $u_1,\dots,u_k \in L^{k+1}(\mathbb{T}^d)$, $d\geq 1$, $k\geq 2$ and, 
\begin{align}
    \beta \geq 1 + d\left(\tfrac{k-1}{k+1}\right)\label{Intersection:Lk1:multilinear:beta_assumption}
\end{align}
there is the following estimate, 
\begin{align*}
    \Big\|\phi_\beta(D_x)\prod_{i=1}^k u_i \Big\|_{L^{k+1}} \lesssim \prod_{i=1}^k\left\|u_i\right\|_{L^{k+1}}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Again, for readability, we prove the case for $u_1=\dots=u_k$. As we did in the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:multilinear_estimate} we note that  $\phi_\beta(D_x)$ smooths $\beta-1$ derivatives
\begin{align}
    \left\|\phi_\beta(D_x)u^k\right\|_{L^{k+1}} \sim \left\|u^k\right\|_{W^{1-\beta,k+1}}.\label{Intersection:Lk1:multilinear:smoothing}
\end{align}
Now, by the Sobolev embedding theorem, we see that $L^{\frac{k+1}{k}}(\mathbb{T}^d) \hookrightarrow W^{1-\beta,k+1}(\mathbb{T}^d)$ as,
\begin{align*}
    \beta - 1 - \tfrac{dk}{k+1} \geq -\tfrac{d}{k+1} \iff \beta \geq 1+ d\left(\tfrac{k-1}{k+1}\right)
\end{align*}
which is satisfied by assumption \eqref{Intersection:Lk1:multilinear:beta_assumption}. Therefore, returning to \eqref{Intersection:Lk1:multilinear:smoothing},
\begin{align*}
    \left\|\phi_\beta(D_x)u^k\right\|_{L^{k+1}} \lesssim \left\|u^k\right\|_{L^{\frac{k+1}{k}}} = \left\|u\right\|^k_{L^{k+1}}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{Lk1_local_lemma}
    If $d\geq 1$, $k\geq 2$,  $u_0\in L^{k+1}(\mathbb{T}^d)$ and,
    \begin{align*}
         1+ d\left(\tfrac{k-1}{k+1}\right) \leq \beta \leq 2
    \end{align*}
    then \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} is locally well-posed in $L^{k+1}(\mathbb{T}^d)$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
As noted by Genovese \textit{et al.} in the work on the transport of Gaussian measures for the fractional BBM equation \cite{genovese2023}, one may Taylor expand $S(t) = e^{t\phi_\beta(D_x)}$ to obtain
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|S(t) u\right\|_{L^{k+1}} \lesssim e^t \left\|u\right\|_{L^{k+1}}.
    \end{align*}
    Using this and Lemma \ref{Intersection:Local:multilinear_lem}, one obtains the result via the standard fixed point procedure.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Using Lemma \ref{Lk1_local_lemma} and the uniform boundedness of the $L^{k+1}(\mathbb{T}^d)$ norm of the solution as provided by Lemma \ref{Global:critical:general:Lk1_control}, we obtain our global result by the standard iteration argument.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{Appendix:lem:strichartz}
    Let $d\geq 1$, $p\geq 2$ and $u_0\in L^2(\mathbb{T}^d)$. Then, there exists $C>0$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|\left\|u_0^\omega \right\|_{L^p(\mathbb{T}^d)}\right\|_{L^p(\Omega)} \leq C \sqrt{p}|\mathbb{T}^d|^{1/p} \left\|u_0\right\|_{L^2(\mathbb{T}^d)}.
    \end{align*}
    Furthermore, this implies that there exists $\Sigma \subset \Omega$ such that $\mathbb{P}(\Sigma) = 1$ and that for all $\omega \in \Sigma$, $u_0^\omega \in L^p(\mathbb{T}^d)$.\footnote{This is simply the definition of the almost sure membership $u_0^\omega \in L^p(\mathbb{T}^d)$, but we explicitly include it for the proof of Corollary \ref{cor:almost_sure_global}}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
See \cite[Section 4.2.2]{gubinelli2019} for a proof.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Fixing $u_0\in L^2(\mathbb{T}^d)$, we randomise according to \eqref{Intro:data_randomisation} to obtain $u_0^\omega$. Now, using Lemma \ref{Appendix:lem:strichartz}, we ensure that there exists a set $\Sigma \subset \Omega$ such that $\mathbb{P}(\Sigma) = 1$ and $u_0^\omega \in L^{k+1}(\mathbb{T}^d)$, for all $\omega \in \Sigma$. For these values of $\omega$, we may then apply Theorem \ref{Intersection:Lk1:local:thm} to obtain a unique, stable, global solution $u^\omega(t)$ to \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} with initial data $u_0^\omega$. With this, we yield the almost sure global well-posedness of \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} in $L^2(\Omega)$. By the persistence of regularity of the solution map, as afforded by our local well-posedness on the $H^s$ scale (Theorem \ref{Local_theory:lwp_main}), we extend the almost sure global result to $H^s$ for any $s\geq 0$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{THM:Local:endpoint}
    Let $\mu \in \{-1,1\}$. Then \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE} is locally well-posed in $H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{LEM:Local:endpoint:subsequence}
    Let $(u_{n,0})_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\subset H^1(\mathbb{T})$ such that $u_{n,0}\to u_0$ in $H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$ and denote~$u_n \in C([0,T];H^1(\mathbb{T}))$ to be the global solution of \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE}. There exists a subsequence $u_{n_m}$ and a function $u \in C_{w}([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap C\left([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T})\right)$ such that,
    \begin{align}
        &u_{n_{m}} \longrightarrow u \text{\, weakly-$\star$ in \,} L^\infty\left([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})\right)\label{Local:endpoint:main_weak_star}\\
         &u_{n_m}\longrightarrow u \text{\, strongly in \,} C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))\label{Local:endpoint:main_strong}.
    \end{align}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
We first note that \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE} is globally well-posed in $H^1(\mathbb{T})$ by Proposition \ref{Regime_3:proposition_result}. Now, Let $(u_{n,0})_{n\in \mathbb{N}} \subset H^1(\mathbb{T})$ such that $u_{n,0}\to u_0$ in $H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$. Moreover, for each $n\in \mathbb{N}$ denote $u_n(t) \in C([0,T] ; H^1(\mathbb{R}))$ to be the evolution of $u_{0,n}$ under \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE}  as obtained in Proposition \ref{Regime_3:proposition_result}. By Lemma  \ref{Global:lemma:Conservervation} the $H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$ norm of $u_n$ is conserved, i.e.
    \begin{align}
        \left\|u_n\right\|_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})} =  \mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_{0,n})\label{Local:endpoint:conservation_claim}
    \end{align}
    for any $t\in [0,T]$. Therefore, $u_{n}$ is bounded in $C([0,T] ; H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}))$. Seeking to obtain uniform control of $\partial_t u_n$ in $L^2$ we first multiply \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE} by $\partial_t u_n$ and integrate yielding,
    \begin{align*}
        \int_\mathbb{T} |\partial_t u_n|^2\, dx = -\int_\mathbb{T} (\partial_t u_n) \partial_x \left(1+D_x\right)^{-1} \left(u_n + \mu\frac{u_n^3}{3}\right)\, dx.
    \end{align*}
    Using Young's product inequality, we see for all $\varepsilon >0$,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|\partial_t u_n\right\|^2_{L^2} &\leq \varepsilon\int_\mathbb{T} \left|\partial_t u_n\right|^2 \, dx + \frac{1}{2\varepsilon} \left\|\phi_\beta(D_x) u_n\right\|^2_{L^2} + \frac{1}{2\varepsilon} \big\|\phi_\beta(D_x) \frac{u_n^3}{3}\big\|_{L^2}.
    \end{align*}
    Setting $\varepsilon=1/2$, we get that,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|\partial_t u_n\right\|^2_{L^2} \lesssim \left\|\phi_\beta(D_x)u_n\right\|_{L^2}^2 + \left\|\phi_\beta(D_x) u_n^3\right\|_{L^2}^2\lesssim \left\|u_n\right\|^2_{L^2} + \left\|u_n^3\right\|^2_{L^2}
    \end{align*}
    where in the final line we have used the $L^2$ boundedness of $\phi_\beta(D_x)$. Now, using the Sobolev embedding we find a uniform bound,
    \begin{align*}
         \left\|\partial_t u_n\right\|^2_{L^2} = \left\|u_n\right\|^2_{L^2} + \left\|u_n\right\|^6_{L^6}\lesssim \left\|u_n\right\|^2_{H^{1/2}} + \left\|u_n\right\|^6_{H^{1/2}} \leq  \mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_{0,n}) + \mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_{0,n})^3.
    \end{align*}
    By our above estimates, we see that $u_n$ is bounded in $L^\infty([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}))$ and that $\partial_t u_n$ is bounded in $L^\infty([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$. Using the boundedness of $u_n$, Banach-Alaoglue guarantees that there exists a subsequence $u_{n_k}$ and a function $u \in L^{\infty}\left([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})\right)$ such that,
    \begin{align}
        u_{n_{k}} \longrightarrow u \text{\, weakly-$\star$ in \,} L^\infty\left([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})\right)\label{Local:endpoint:weak_star_condition}.
    \end{align}
    Furthermore, by Rellich-Kondrakov, we know that $H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$ is compactly embedded in $L^2(\mathbb{T})$. Therefore, we have the following chain of embeddings,
    \begin{align*}
        H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})  \xhookrightarrow{\text{compact}} L^2(\mathbb{T}) \xhookrightarrow{\text{continuous}} L^2(\mathbb{T}).
    \end{align*}
    Applying the Aubin-Lion Lemma (see \cite[Theorem 2.5.16]{boyer2013}), the space $W$ defined,
    \begin{align*}
        W = \left\{u \in L^{\infty}\left([0,T]; H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})\right): \partial_t u \in L^\infty\left([0,T] ;L^2(\mathbb{T})\right)\right\}
    \end{align*} 
    is compactly embedded in $C\left([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T})\right)$. Clearly $\left(u_n\right)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\subset W$ meaning that $u_n$ is relatively compact in $C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$. Therefore, we can extract a subsequence $u_{n_j}$ and a limit $v\in C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$ such that,
    \begin{align}
        u_{n_j}\longrightarrow v \text{\, strongly in \,} C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T})).\label{Local:endpoint:strong_condition}
    \end{align}
    Now, to prove that $u\equiv v$ we take the subsequence $u_{n_k}$ satisfying \eqref{Local:endpoint:weak_star_condition} and index it via $j$ where $u_{n_j}$ satisfies \eqref{Local:endpoint:strong_condition}. The sequence $u_{n_{k_j}}$ will satisfy both of these properties and since strong convergence implies weak-$\star$ convergence, and $L^2(\mathbb{T})$ is Hausdorff, the weak-$\star$ limit must be unique, meaning $u\equiv v$. Defining a new index $m$ such that $u_{n_m} = u_{n_{k_j}}$ we get that there exists a function $u\in L^{\infty}\left([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})\right)\cap C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$ such that, \eqref{Local:endpoint:main_weak_star} and \eqref{Local:endpoint:strong_condition} are satisfied. Since $H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$ is seperable, reflexive and embedded continuously into $L^2(\mathbb{T})$, it follows from standard regularity results that $u \in C_w\left([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})\right)\cap C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$ (see, \cite[Lemma 2.5.9]{boyer2013}).
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{Local:endpoint:Lem:existence}
    For initial data $u_0\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$, there exists a local weak solution $u$ to \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE} such that $u\in C_w([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Let $u_{n_m}$ be the subsequence obtained in \ref{LEM:Local:endpoint:subsequence}. We have that for all $k\in \mathbb{N}$ and all $v\in C^\infty_c(\mathbb{T})$,
    \begin{align*}
        \int_0^T \int_\mathbb{T} v \partial_t u_{n_m} + v\partial_t D_x u_{n_m} + v\partial_x \left(u_{n_m} + \mu\frac{u_{n_m}^3}{3}\right)\, dx\, dt=0.
    \end{align*}
    Integrating by parts we obtain,
    \begin{align}
        \int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{T}} u_{n_m} \partial_t v + u_{n_m}\partial_t D_x v + \left(u_{n_m}+\mu\frac{u_{n_m}^3}{3}\right)\partial_x v \, dx\, dt =0\label{Local:endpoint:integration_by_parts}
    \end{align}
    Now, by the weak-$\star$ convergence of $u_{n_m}$ we obtain a limit for our linear terms,
   \begin{align*}
    \lim_{m\to \infty}\int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{T}} u_{n_m} \partial_t v + u_{n_m}\partial_t D_x v + u_{n_m}\partial_x v \, dx\, dt = \int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{T}} u \partial_t v + u\partial_t D_x v + u\partial_x v \, dx\, dt.
   \end{align*}
   For the nonlinear term, we require the strong convergence due to the product giving us,
   \begin{align*}
    \lim_{m\to \infty}\int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{T}} \mu\frac{u_{n_m}^3}{3} \partial_x v\, dx = \int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{T}} \mu\frac{u^3}{3} \partial_x v\, dx.
   \end{align*}
   Returning these to \eqref{Local:endpoint:integration_by_parts} we get that,
   \begin{align*}
    \int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{T}} u \partial_t v + u\partial_t D_x v + \left(u+\mu\frac{u^3}{3}\right)\partial_x v \, dx\, dt =0
   \end{align*}
   for all $v\in C^\infty(\mathbb{T})$, meaning that $u$ is a weak solution to \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{Local:endpoint:commutator_estimate}
    for $1\leq p<\infty$ and $1/q + 1/p = 1/r$ with $p$ and $r$ valid Lebesgue exponents, there is the following commutator estimate,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|\phi_\beta(D_x)(uv) - u\phi_\beta(D_x)v - v\phi_\beta(D_x)u\right\|_{L^p} \lesssim \left\|u\right\|_{L^q}\left\|v\right\|_{L^r}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
See \cite[Lemma 1.3]{he2018}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{Lem:Local:endpoint:uniqueness}
        The weak solution obtained to \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE} in Lemma \ref{Local:endpoint:Lem:existence} is unique in $C_w([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$ for fixed initial data $u_0\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$ and some $T := T(u_0) >0$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Assume that there exists weak solutions $u,v \in C_w([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$ to \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE} with the same initial data $u_0\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})$. We first note that we can obtain uniform in time bounds on the $H^{1/2}$ norms of $u$ and $v$. For $u$ this can be done via the weak approximating sequence of $H^1(\mathbb{T})$ solutions $u_{n_m}$ and the weak lower semi-continuity of the norm,
        \begin{align}
            \sup_{t\in [0,T]}\left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^{1/2}} &\leq \liminf_{m\to \infty} \left\|u_{n_m}\right\|_{H^{1/2}} = \liminf_{m\to \infty} \left\|u_{n_m,0}\right\|_{H^{1/2}} = \left\|u_0\right\|_{H^{1/2}} = \mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0)\label{Local:endpoint:unif_bound_u}
        \end{align}
        where we used the conservation \eqref{Local:endpoint:conservation_claim}. Since we lack an approximating sequence for $v$, we instead use the uniform boundedness of weakly continuous functions on compact intervals to obtain,
        \begin{align}
            \sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|v(t)\right\|_{H^{1/2}}&\leq C_{v_0,T}\label{Local:endpoint:unif_bound_v}.
        \end{align}
        for some constant $C_{v_0,T}>0$. Now, define
        \begin{align*}
            g(t) \coloneqq \left\|u(t)-v(t)\right\|^2_{L^2}
        \end{align*}
        and $w\coloneqq u-v$. Taking the time derivative of $g$, aiming to use Gr\"onwall's inequality we see that,
        \begin{align}
            g'(t) = 2\int_\mathbb{T} (u-v)(\partial_t u - \partial_t v)\, dx &= -2\int_{\mathbb{T}}(u-v)\phi_\beta(D_x)\left(u+\mu\frac{u^3}{3} - v - \mu\frac{v^3}{3}\right)\, dx\notag\\
            &= -2\int_\mathbb{T} w\phi_\beta(D_x)w + \mu w\phi_\beta(D_x)\left(\frac{u^3}{3} - \frac{v^3}{3}\right)\, dx\notag\\
            &= -\mu\frac{2}{3} \int_\mathbb{T} w \phi_\beta(D_x)\left(w\left(u^2 + uv + v^2\right)\right)\, dx\label{Local:endpoint:uniqueness:ipb}
        \end{align}
        where in the last inequality we used integration by parts. Now, defining $A := A(u,v) = u^2 +uv + v^2$ and $R := R(u,v) = \phi_\beta(D_x)(wA) -A\phi_\beta(D_x)w - w\phi_\beta(D_x) A$ we can split \eqref{Local:endpoint:uniqueness:ipb} like so,
        \begin{align}
            g'(t) &= -\mu \frac{2}{3}\int_\mathbb{T} w^2\phi_\beta(D_x)A \, dx -\mu \frac{2}{3}\int_\mathbb{T} wA \phi_\beta(D_x) w\, dx -\mu \frac{2}{3}\int_\mathbb{T} w R\, dx\notag\\
            &= \text{Term 1} + \text{Term 2} + \text{Term 3}\label{Local:endpoint:term_split}.
        \end{align}
        Before bounding each of these terms seperately it will be useful to have a bound on the $L^p(\mathbb{T})$ norm of $A$ for some $1\leq p<\infty$. Using H\"older's and Young's product inequality we get that
        \begin{align*}
            \left\|A\right\|_{L^p} &\lesssim \left\|u\right\|^2_{L^{2p}} + \left\|v\right\|^2_{L^{2p}}.
        \end{align*}
        Now using Lemma \ref{Lem:Trudinger_type} we obtain,
        \begin{align*}
            \left\|A\right\|_{L^p}\lesssim p\left(\left\|u\right\|^2_{H^{1/2}} + \left\|v\right\|^2_{H^{1/2}}\right).
        \end{align*}
        Finally, \ref{Local:endpoint:unif_bound_u} and \eqref{Local:endpoint:unif_bound_v} we obtain the uniform bound,
        \begin{align}
            \left\|A\right\|_{L^p} \lesssim p\max(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0)^2, C_{v_0,T}^2)\label{Local:endpoint:A_unif_bound}.
        \end{align}
        We now go through the terms in \eqref{Local:endpoint:term_split}, obtaining our upper bounds.
        \begin{itemize}
            \item \underline{Term 1:} Note that  $w^2 = w^{2(1-1/p)}w^{2/p}$.
            \begin{align*}
                \left|\text{Term 1}\right| &\lesssim \big\| w^{2(1-1/p)}\big\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}\big\|w^{2/p}\phi_\beta(D_x)A\big\|_{L^p}\\
                &\leq \left\|w\right\|^{2(1-1/p)}_{L^2}\left\|w\right\|^{2/p}_{L^4} \left\|\phi_\beta(D_x)A\right\|_{L^{2p}}\\
                &\leq g(t)^{1-1/p}\left(\left\|u\right\|^{2/p}_{L^4} + \left\|v\right\|_{L^4}^{2/p}\right)\left\|\phi_\beta(D_x)A\right\|_{L^{2p}}.
            \end{align*}
            Using the $L^p$ boundedness of $\phi_\beta(D_x)$ and \eqref{Local:endpoint:A_unif_bound} we obtain,
            \begin{align*}
                \left|\text{Term 1}\right| &\lesssim p\max(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0)^2, C_{v_0,T}^2) g(t)^{1-1/p} \left(\left\|u\right\|^{2/p}_{L^4} + \left\|v\right\|_{L^4}^{2/p}\right).
            \end{align*}
            Now Using Lemma \ref{Lem:Trudinger_type} with our uniform bounds \eqref{Local:endpoint:unif_bound_u} and \eqref{Local:endpoint:unif_bound_v},
            \begin{align}
                \left|\text{Term 1}\right| &\lesssim p\max(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0), C_{v_0,T})^{2(1+1/p)} g(t)^{1-1/p}\label{Local:endpoint:Term_1_bound}.
            \end{align}
            \item \underline{Term 2:} Here we note that $w = w^{1-2/p}w^{2/p}$
            \begin{align*}
                \big|\text{Term 2}\big| &\lesssim \big\|w^{1-2/p} \phi_\beta(D_x)w\big\|_{L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}\big\|w^{2/p}A\big\|_{L^p}\\
                &\lesssim \big\|w^{1-2/p}\big\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p-2}}}\left\|\phi_\beta(D_x)w\right\|_{L^2}\left(\left\|u\right\|^{2/p}_{L^4} + \left\|v\right\|_{L^4}^{2/p}\right)\left\|A\right\|_{L^{2p}}\\
                &\lesssim g(t)^{1-1/p} \left(\left\|u\right\|^{2/p}_{L^4} + \left\|v\right\|_{L^4}^{2/p}\right)\left\|A\right\|_{L^{2p}}.
            \end{align*}
            Using \eqref{Local:endpoint:A_unif_bound}, Lemma \ref{Lem:Trudinger_type} and our uniform bounds \eqref{Local:endpoint:unif_bound_u} and \eqref{Local:endpoint:unif_bound_v} we get,
            \begin{align}
                \left|\text{Term 2}\right| \lesssim p\max(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0), C_{v_0,T})^{2(1+1/p)} g(t)^{1-1/p}\label{Local:endpoint:Term_2_bound}.
            \end{align}
            \item \underline{Term 3:} Using the commutator estimate, Lemma \ref{Local:endpoint:commutator_estimate} we get that for $\frac{1}{q} = \frac{1}{p}(1-\frac{2}{p})$ and $\frac{1}{r} = \frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{q}$,
            \begin{align*}
                \left|\text{Term 3}\right| &\lesssim \left\|w\right\|_{L^2}\left\|A\right\|_{L^q}\left\|w\right\|_{L^r}\\
                &\lesssim \left\|w\right\|_{L^2}\left\|A\right\|_{L^q}\left\|w\right\|^{1-2/p}_{L^2}\left\|w\right\|_{L^p}^{\frac{p}{2}}\\
                &\leq \left\|w\right\|_{L^2}\left\|A\right\|_{L^q}\left\|w\right\|^{1-2/p}_{L^2}\left(\left\|u\right\|_{L^p}^{\frac{p}{2}}+ \left\|v\right\|_{L^p}^{\frac{p}{2}}\right)\\
                &\lesssim qp^{1/p}\max\left(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0),C_{v_0,T}\right)^{2(1+1/p)}g(t)^{1-1/p}.
            \end{align*}
            Now taking $p>2$ large enough such that,
            \begin{align*}
                 qp^{1/p} &= p^{1/p}\frac{p}{1-\frac{2}{p}}\lesssim p
            \end{align*}
            we obtain,
            \begin{align}
                \left|\text{Term 3}\right| &\lesssim p \max\left(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0),C_{v_0,T}\right)^{2(1+1/p)}g(t)^{1-1/p}.\label{Local:endpoint:Term_3_bound}
            \end{align}
        \end{itemize}
        Now, combining \eqref{Local:endpoint:Term_1_bound}, \eqref{Local:endpoint:Term_2_bound} and \eqref{Local:endpoint:Term_3_bound} in \eqref{Local:endpoint:term_split} we get that for some constant $C>0$,
        \begin{align*}
            g'(t) &\leq  p C\max\left(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0),C_{v_0,T}\right)^{2(1+1/p)}g(t)^{1-1/p}.
        \end{align*}
        Integrating this we yield,
        \begin{align*}
            g(t) \leq \left(C\max\left(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0),C_{v_0,T}\right)^2\right)^p.
        \end{align*}
        For this upper bound to go to zero as $p\to \infty$ we require that $C\max\left(\mathscr{E}_{1,1}(u_0),C_{v_0,T}\right)^2 <1$. Since these constants depend only on our initial data however, for any $u_0$, there exists a constant $T>0$ such that on $[0,T]$, $g(t) = 0$. Therefore, we obtain uniqueness of our weak solution $u\in C_w([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap C([0,T];L^2(\mathbb{T}))$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{Local:endpoint:failure_remark}
        The above method fails for the quintic and higher powers of the regurarised BO equation. One obtains a multiplicative factor of $p$ in the bound for $g(t)$, forcing the constant $C$ to depend on $p$ for the limit to evaluate to zero. This destroys the argument, indicating that new ideas are needed.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:Local:endpoint:continuity}
        The unique weak solution $u$ obtained in Lemma \ref{Lem:Local:endpoint:uniqueness} is in $C([0,T];H^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}))$ and is continuous with respect to the initial data $u_0$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
By the weak lower semi-continuity of the norm  and the conservation \eqref{Local:endpoint:conservation_claim} of the $H^1$ initial data we obtain,
        \begin{align*}
            \left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^{1/2}} \leq \liminf_{m\to \infty}\left\|u_{n_m}(t)\right\|_{H^{1/2}} \leq \left\|u_0\right\|_{H^{1/2}}.
        \end{align*}
        Furthermore, by time reversal of \eqref{cubic_rbo_PDE} we get that,
        \begin{align*}
            \left\|u(0)\right\|_{H^{1/2}} \leq \left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^{1/2}}.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, by the uniqueness of $u$, we know that $\left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^{1/2}} = \left\|u_0\right\|_{H^{1/2}}$. From this we can see that $u(t)$ is continuous in norm as $\left\|u(t)\right\|_{H^{1/2}} = \left\|u(s)\right\|_{H^{1/2}}$ for any $s<t<T$. Therefore, $u(t)$ is continuous in norm and weakly continuous, meaning it must be strongly continuous. The same reasoning can be used to infer that $u_0\mapsto u(t)$ is strongly continuous as $t\to 0$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Combine Lemmas \ref{Local:endpoint:Lem:existence}, \ref{Lem:Local:endpoint:uniqueness} and \ref{lem:Local:endpoint:continuity}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
By Proposition \ref{THM:Local:endpoint} and the conservation of the $H^{1/2}$ norm, \eqref{Conservation:H1_like} we obtain a global solution via the standard iteration argument.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{Prop_norm_integer}
    Let $d\geq 1$, $\mu \in \{-1,1\}$, $1\leq \beta \leq 2$, $k\geq 3$ and $s<0$. The following formulations are equivalent:
    \begin{itemize}
    \item \underline{Norm inflation everywhere:} For any $u_0\in H^s$ and $\varepsilon>0$, there exists a solution $u_\varepsilon$ to \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} on $t\in (0,\varepsilon)$ such that,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|u_\varepsilon(0) - u_0\right\|_{H^s} < \varepsilon \quad \text{and} \quad \left\|u_\varepsilon(t)\right\|_{H^s} > \varepsilon^{-1}.
    \end{align*}
    \item \underline{Quantitative norm inflation everywhere:} For any $u_0\in H^s$ and $n\in \mathbb{N}$, there exists a solution $u_n$ to \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} and $t_n \in (0,1/n)$ such that,
    \begin{align}
        \left\|u_n(0) - u_0\right\|_{H^s} < \tfrac{1}{n} \quad \text{and} \quad \|u_n(t_n)\|_{H^s}>n \label{integer_norm_inflation}
    \end{align}
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
See \cite[Proposition 3.1]{oh2017}
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{Thm:quant_norm_inflation}
    Let $k\geq 2 $, $1\leq \beta\leq 2$, $\mu \in \{-1,1\}$, $s<0$ and $d\geq 1$. Then \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} undergoes quantitative norm inflation everywhere.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{Illposdness:local_FL}
    For data $u_0\in \mathscr{F}L^1(\mathbb{T}^d)$, there exists $T \lesssim_\beta k^{-1}\left(2\left\|u_0\right\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1}\right)^{1-k}$ and a unique solution $u\in C\left([0,T];\mathscr{F}L^1\right)$ to \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq}.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
The proof follows by the algebra property of $\mathscr{F}L^1(\mathbb{T}^d)$ and the unitarity of the propagator $S_\beta(t)$ in $\mathscr{F}L^1$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
(i) Given a partially ordered set $\mathscr{T}$ with partial order $\leq$, we say that $b\in \mathscr{T}$ with $b\leq a$ and $b\neq a$ is a \emph{child} of $a\in \mathscr{T}$, if $b\leq c \leq a$ implies $c=a$ or $c=b$. If the latter condition holds we also say that $a$ is a \emph{child} of $b$.
   
  (ii) A tree $\mathscr{T}$ is a finite partially ordered set, satisfying the following properties:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item Let $a_1,a_2,a_3,a_4\in \mathscr{T}$. If $a_4 \leq a_2\leq a_1$ and $a_4 \leq a_3 \leq a_1$, then we have $a_2\leq a_3$ or $a_3\leq a_2$,
    \item A node $a\in \mathscr{T}$ is called \emph{terminal}, if it has no child. A \emph{non-terminal} node $a\in \mathscr{T}$ is a node with exactly $k$ children.
    \item There exists a maximimal element $r\in \mathscr{T}$, the rood node of the tree, such that $a\leq r$ for all $a\in \mathscr{T}$,
    \item $\mathscr{T}$ consists of the disjoint union of $\mathscr{T}^0$ and $\mathscr{T}^\infty$, where $\mathscr{T}^0$ and $\mathscr{T}^\infty$ denote the collections of non-terminal nodes and terminal nodes, respectively.
  \end{itemize}
  (iii) Let $T_k(j)$ denote the set of all trees with $j$ non-terminal nodes.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem: T_k(j) bound}
        Let $T_k(j)$ be the set of $k$-ary trees on $j$ generations. There exists $C_0 > 0$ such that,
        \[\# T_k(j) \leq C^j_0.\]
        for all $j \in \mathbb{N}$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
See \cite[Lemma 2.2]{forlano2020a} or \cite[Lemma 2.3]{oh2017} for the $k=3$ case.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{Illposedness_definitions}
    We denote,
    \begin{align*}
         e_{1,d} &\coloneqq  (1,0,\dots,0) \in \mathbb{Z}^d & b_d &\coloneqq \left\{\xi \in \mathbb{Z}^d: |\xi| \leq 1\right\}
    \end{align*}
    and define the following variables where a subscript denotes dependance,
     \begin{align*}
        N &\coloneqq N_n \gg 1 &
        A &\coloneqq A_N  \gg 1 &
        R &\coloneqq R_N &
        Q_{A,d} &\coloneqq \left[-\tfrac{A}{2},\tfrac{A}{2}\right)^d \subset \mathbb{Z}^d
        \end{align*}
        defined such that
\begin{align}
    RA \gg \|u_0\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1} \quad \text{and} \quad A \ll N \label{A,d,n,R_rel}.
\end{align}
Lastly, define we define,
\begin{align*}
    f_{s,d}(A) \coloneqq \begin{cases}
1, & \text{if } s < -\tfrac{d}{2}, \\[4pt]
(\log A)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}, & \text{if } s = -\tfrac{d}{2}, \\[4pt]
A^{\tfrac{d}{2} + s}, & \text{if } s > -\tfrac{d}{2}.
\end{cases}
\end{align*}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
There exists $C>0$ such that  for all $\psi \in \mathscr{F}L^p(\mathbb{T}^d) \cap \mathscr{F}L^1(\mathbb{T}^d)$ with $1\leq p\leq \infty$ and $j\in \mathbb{N}$,
\begin{align}
    \| \Xi_j(\psi)(t)\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1} &\leq C^j t^j\|\psi\|^{(k-1)j+1}_{\mathscr{F}L^1}\label{Xibbm-Fl1-est}\\
    \| \Xi_j(\psi)(t)\|_{\mathscr{F}L^\infty} &\leq C^j t^j \|\psi\|^{(k-1)j-1}_{\mathscr{F}L^1}\|\psi\|^2_{L^2}\label{Xibbm-flinf}\\
    \left\| \Xi_j(u_0 + \psi)(t) - \Xi_j(\psi)(t)\right\|_{\mathscr{F}L^p} &\leq C^j t^j\|u_0\|_{\mathscr{F}L^p}\left(\|u_0\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1}^{(k-1)j+1} + \left\|\psi\right\|^{(k-1)j+1}_{\mathscr{F}L^1}\right) \label{Multi-Xi-est-BBm}.
\end{align}
Furthermore, for $\psi_n$ as defined in \eqref{phin-bbm-def} and $s<0$ we have that,
\begin{align}
         \|\psi_n\|_{H^s} \sim RA^{d/2}N^s \quad \text{and} \quad \|\psi_n\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1}\sim RA^d \label{phi_growth}.
    \end{align}
With  $u_{0,n} = u_0 + \psi_n$ it also holds that
    \begin{align}
         \|u_{0,n} - u_0\|_{H^s} &\lesssim RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s\label{est: BBM u_0,n - u_0}\\
        \left\|\Xi_0(u_{0,n})\right\|_{H^s}&\lesssim 1+ RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s\label{est: BBM Xi_0}\\
         \left\|\Xi_1(u_{0,n}) - \Xi_1(\psi_n)(t)\right\|_{H^s} &\lesssim t\|u_0\|_{L^2}R^{k-1} A^{(k-1)d}\label{est: BBM Xi_1 diff}\\
         \|\Xi_j(u_{0,n}^{})(t)\|_{H^s} &\leq  C^jt^j(RA^d)^{(k-1)j}\left(\|u_0\|_{L^2} + Rf_s(A)\right)\label{Xi-Hs-up-bd}.
    \end{align}
Lastly for some fixed constants $c>0$ and $M\geq 1$,
\begin{align}
    \|\langle\xi\rangle^s\|_{\ell^2(B_{cMA}(\xi))} &\lesssim Mf_s(A)\label{large_factor_xi_control}\\
    \|\langle\xi\rangle^s\|_{\ell^2(B_{cMA}(\xi))} &\sim \|\langle\xi\rangle^s\|_{L^2(B_{cMA}(\xi)\setminus b)}\label{eq: annulus_control_xi}.
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
The proof of \eqref{Xibbm-Fl1-est} and \eqref{Xibbm-flinf} follow almost identically from \cite[Lemma 2.3]{forlano2020a}. \eqref{Multi-Xi-est-BBm} follows from \cite[Lemma 2.6]{oh2017} and \eqref{phi_growth} follows simply from expanding the definition of $\psi_n$. \eqref{est: BBM u_0,n - u_0}, \eqref{est: BBM Xi_0}, \eqref{est: BBM Xi_1 diff} and \eqref{Xi-Hs-up-bd} follow almost identically from \cite[Lemma 3.2]{forlano2020a}. Lastly, \eqref{large_factor_xi_control} and \eqref{eq: annulus_control_xi} follow from evaluating the corresponding sums.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{Power-series-convergence}
    Let $u_{0,n}^{} = u_0 + \psi_n$ with corresponding solution to \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} $u_n$. The following power series converges absolutely.
    \begin{align*}
        u_n = \sum_{j=0}^\infty \Xi_j(u_{0,n}^{})
    \end{align*}
    on $[0,T]$ for,
    \begin{align*}
        T \lesssim \left(\|u_0\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1} + RA\right)^{k-1} \sim \left(RA\right)^{1-k}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
See \cite[Lemma 2.3]{forlano2020a} for a proof of convergence of a similar power series.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{Lem: cube conv ineq}
    Let $d\geq 1$. Then there exists $c_d$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbbm{1}_{a+Q_{A,d}} * \mathbbm{1}_{b+Q_{A,d}}(\xi) \geq c_d A^d \mathbbm{1}_{a+b+Q_{A,d}}(\xi)
    \end{align*}
    for all $a,b,\xi \in \mathbb{Z}^d$ and $A\geq 1$.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{Lem: LOWERBD}
    Let $\psi_n$ be defined as in \eqref{phin-bbm-def} and $s<0$. For $0<t\ll 1$, we have,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|\Pi_{Q_{A,d}}\Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\|_{H^s} &\gtrsim  tR^k A^{d(k-1)} f_s(A).
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Let $\omega(\xi) = \widehat{\phi_\beta}(\xi)$. We recall,
    \begin{align*}
        \Xi_1(\psi_n)(t) = \int_0^t S_\beta(t-t')\psi_\beta(D_x)\left(S_\beta(t') \psi_n(t')\right)^k \, dt'.
    \end{align*}
    Taking the Fourier transform and bounding it's modulus below by it's real component we get that,
    \begin{align}
         &\left|\mathscr{F}\left\{ \Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\}(\xi)\right|\notag\\
          &\hphantom{X}\geq \Big|\omega(\xi)\Big|\sum_{\substack{\xi = \xi_1+\cdots+\xi_k \\ \xi \in \mathbb{Z}^d}} \prod_{i=1}^k \widehat{\psi_n}(\xi_i)\operatorname{Re} \int_0^t \exp\Big(-it' \Big(\omega(\xi) - \sum_{i=1}^k \omega(\xi_i)\Big)\Big)\, dt'\label{eq:FT_for_BBM_Xi_1}
    \end{align}
   where we have used that $e^{t\omega(\xi)}$ is on the unit circle and each of the $\widehat{\psi_n}$ functions are real valued. We note that given $\xi_i \in \operatorname{supp} \widehat{\psi_n}$, $|\xi_i|\sim N$. Therefore, we have that $|\xi|= \left|\xi_1+\dots+ \xi_k\right| \sim N$ and resultantly for $0\leq t' \ll 1$
    \begin{align*}
        \Big|t' \Big[\omega(\xi) - \sum_{i=1}^{k} \omega(\xi_i)\Big]\Big| \ll 1.
    \end{align*}
    Now, note that when $|x|\leq \tfrac{\pi}{3}$, $\cos(x) \geq \tfrac{1}{2}$. As such, for $0\leq t' \ll 1$,
    \begin{align*}
         \operatorname{Re} \int_0^t \exp\Big(-it'\Big[\omega(\xi) - \sum_{i=1}^{k} \omega(\xi_i)\Big]\Big) \, dt' &=\int_0^t \cos\Big(-t'\Big[\omega(\xi) - \sum_{i=1}^{k} \omega(\xi_i)\Big]\Big) \, dt'\geq \frac{1}{2}t.
    \end{align*}
    Using this in \eqref{eq:FT_for_BBM_Xi_1},
    \begin{align*}
         \left|\mathscr{F}\left\{ \Xi_1(\psi_n)(t)\right\}(\xi)\right| &\geq t\frac{\left|\omega(\xi)\right|}{2}\sum_{\substack{\xi = \xi_1+\cdots+\xi_k \\ \xi \in \mathbb{Z}^d}} \prod_{i=1}^k \widehat{\psi_n}(\xi_i)
         =   t\tfrac{\left|\omega(\xi)\right|}{2}\big(\widehat{\psi_n}*\dots* \widehat{\psi_n}\big)(\xi).
    \end{align*}
    Given that a convolution of indicator functions will be positive, we may indicate on a nonempty subset of $\mathbb{Z}^d$ to lower bound this value further. As such, we will indicate on $Q_{A,d} \setminus b$ where $b$ is the set defined in Definition \ref{Illposedness_definitions},
    \begin{align*}
        \left|\mathscr{F}\left\{ \Xi_1(\psi_n)(t)\right\}(\xi)\right|
        &\geq \mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d}\setminus b}(\xi)t \tfrac{|\omega(\xi)|}{2}\big(\widehat{\psi_n}*\dots* \widehat{\psi_n}\big)(\xi)\\
        &\geq\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d}\setminus b}(\xi)t\big(\widehat{\psi_n}*\dots* \widehat{\psi_n}\big)(\xi).
    \end{align*} 
     Where in the last inequality we recall that $\xi \in Q_{A,d}\setminus b$ implies that $|\xi| \geq 1$. Expanding our definition of $\psi_n$, \eqref{phin-bbm-def} we get a sum over all possible combinations of $k$-convolutions of the indicator functions in $\widehat{\psi_n}$,
    \begin{align*}
         \left|\mathscr{F}\left\{ \Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\}(\xi)\right| \geq \mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d}\setminus b}(\xi) tR^k\sum_{j_1, \dots,j_k \in \left\{-2,-1,1,2\right\}}\mathbbm{1}_{j_1Ne_{1,d} + Q_{A,d}}*\dots * \mathbbm{1}_{j_kNe_{1,d} +Q_{A,d}}(\xi).
    \end{align*}
    We must now strategically pick a term from this sum such that we can use Lemma \eqref{Lem: cube conv ineq} to end up with a $\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d}}$ term in a lower bound. For $k$ even, we choose the term that alternates between $-Ne_{1,d}+Q_{A,d}$ and $Ne_{1,d} + Q_{A,d}$ like so,
    \begin{align*}
        |\mathscr{F}\left\{ \Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\}(\xi)| & \geq\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d} \setminus b}(\xi) t R^k\left(\mathbbm{1}_{-Ne_{1,d} + Q_{A,d}}*\dots * \mathbbm{1}_{Ne_{1,d} +Q_{A,d}}\right)(\xi)\\
        &\gtrsim tR^k A^{d(k-1)}\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d} \setminus b}(\xi) 
    \end{align*}
    where we have applied Lemma \ref{Lem: cube conv ineq} $k-1$ times. If $k=3$ we choose the following,
    \begin{align*}
        |\mathscr{F}\left\{ \Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\}|(\xi) &\geq\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d} \setminus b}(\xi) t R^k\left(\mathbbm{1}_{-2Ne_{1,d} + Q_{A,d}}* \mathbbm{1}_{Ne_{1,d} + Q_{A,d}} * \mathbbm{1}_{Ne_{1,d} +Q_{A,d}}\right)\\
        &\gtrsim tR^k A^{d(k-1)}\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d} \setminus b}(\xi) .
    \end{align*}
    Where again, we have applied Lemma \ref{Lem: cube conv ineq} $k-1$ times. We can extend this to the more general $k$ odd case by alternating between $-Ne_{1,d} + Q_{A,d}$ and $Ne_{1,d} + Q_{A,d}$ terms until the $k-2$th term, where we choose as we have in the $k=3$ case,
    \begin{align*}
         \left|\mathscr{F}\left\{ \Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\}(\xi)\right|
         &\gtrsim \mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d} \setminus b} t R^kA^{2d}\big(\overbrace{\mathbbm{1}_{-Ne_{1,d} + Q_{A,d}}*\dots * \mathbbm{1}_{Ne_{1,d} +Q_{A,d}}}^{k-3 \text{ terms}} * \mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d}}\big)(\xi)\\
        &\gtrsim t R^k A^{d(k-1)}\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d} \setminus b}.
    \end{align*}   
    Thus, for any $k$, we have,
    \begin{align}
         \left|\mathscr{F}\left\{ \Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\}(\xi)\right| \gtrsim t R^k A^{d(k-1)}\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d} \setminus b}.\label{Illposedness:f_t_lower_bound_xi}
    \end{align}
Taking the $H^s$ norm of $\Pi_{Q_{A,d}}\Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)$,
\begin{align*}
    \left\|\Pi_{Q_{A,d}}\Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\|_{H^s} = \bigg(\sum_{\xi\in Q_{A,d}} \langle \xi\rangle^{2s}\mathscr{F}\left\{\Xi_1^{}(\psi_n)(t)\right\}^2 \bigg)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}&\gtrsim  tR^k A^{d(k-1)} \left\|\langle \xi \rangle^{s}\right\|_{\ell^2_{\xi}(Q_{A,d} \setminus b)}
\end{align*}
 where in the second inequality we used our lower bound \eqref{Illposedness:f_t_lower_bound_xi}. Estimating $\|\langle\xi\rangle^s\|_{\ell^2_\xi(Q_{A,d})}$, we find that for any $0<\varepsilon<A/2$,
    \begin{align*}
        \|\langle\xi\rangle^s\|_{\ell^2_\xi(B_{(1-\varepsilon)A/2\setminus b})}\leq\|\langle\xi\rangle^s\|_{\ell^2_\xi(Q_{A,d}\setminus b)}\leq \|\langle\xi\rangle^s\|_{\ell^2_\xi(B_{(1+\varepsilon)A/2}\setminus b)}
    \end{align*}
    where using \eqref{large_factor_xi_control} and \eqref{eq: annulus_control_xi} gives us that $\|\langle\xi\rangle^s\|_{\ell^2_\xi(Q_{A,d} \setminus b)} \sim f_{s,d}(A)$. This yields the desired result.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{THM:Norm_inf_to_prop}
    Proposition \ref{Thm:quant_norm_inflation} is equivalent to the following properties holding for $n\in \mathbb{N}$.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item[(i)] $RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s \ll \tfrac{1}{n}$,
        \item[(ii)] $R^{k-1}TA^{d(k-1)} \ll 1$,
        \item[(iii)] $TR^kA^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A) \gg n$,
        \item[(iv)] $T \ll 1$
        \item[(v)]\eqref{phi_growth} and $Rf_{s,d}(A)\gg \left\|u_0\right\|_{L^2}$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Assuming (i) and using \eqref{est: BBM u_0,n - u_0}  we get,
\begin{align*}
    \big\|u_{0,n}^{} - u_0\big\|_{H^s} \lesssim RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s \ll \tfrac{1}{n}
\end{align*}
which gives us the first estimate in \eqref{integer_norm_inflation}. Using \eqref{A,d,n,R_rel} and \eqref{phi_growth} we get,
\begin{align*}
    &RA \sim  \|\psi_n\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1}- \|u_{0}\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1} \leq \|u_{0,n}^{}\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1} \leq \|\psi_n\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1}+ \|u_{0}\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1} \sim RA^d\\
    &\hphantom{}\implies \|u_{0,n}^{}\|_{\mathscr{F}L^1}  \sim RA^d.
\end{align*}
Thus, by property (ii) we have that,
\begin{align*}
    T\big\|u_{0,n}^{}\big\|^{k-1}_{\mathscr{F}L^1} \sim TR^{k-1}A^{d(k-1)} \ll 1
\end{align*}
this implies that, $T\lesssim \big\|u_{0,n}^{}\big\|^{1-k}$. Thus, by Theorem \ref{Illposdness:local_FL} we get a local solution $u_n$ to \eqref{Gen-BOBBM-eq} from $[0,T]$ with initial data $u_{0,n}^{}$. Furthermore, by Lemma \ref{Power-series-convergence} we obtain the convergence of the power series on $[0,T]$. By \eqref{est: BBM Xi_0} and property (i) we have that,
\begin{align}
    \big\|\Xi_{0}^{}(u_{0,n}^{})(T)\big\|_{H^s} \lesssim 1+RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s \sim 1.\label{Illposedness:Xi_0:lower_final}
\end{align}
and by Lemma \ref{Lem: LOWERBD} and property (iv) (iii) we get,
\begin{align}
     \left\|\Xi_1^{}\left(\psi_n\right)(T)\right\|_{H^s} \gtrsim TR^kA^{d(k-1)}f_s(A) \gg n\label{proof of prop: Xi_1}.
\end{align}
We can begin to estimate the rest of the power series using \eqref{Xi-Hs-up-bd} and property (v),
\begin{align*}
    \Big\|\sum_{j=2}^\infty \Xi_j(u_{0,n})(T)\Big\|_{H^s} &\leq \sum_{j=2}^\infty \left\|\Xi_j(u_{0,n})(T)\right\|_{H^s}\lesssim \sum_{j=2}^\infty T^jR^{j(k-1)}A^{dj(k-1)}\left(\left\|u_0\right\|_{L^2} + Rf_{s,d}(A)\right)\\
    &\lesssim \sum_{j=2}^\infty T^jR^{j(k-1)+1}A^{dj(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A).
\end{align*}
Now using property (ii),
\begin{align}
    \Big\|\sum_{j=2}^\infty \Xi_j(u_{0,n})(T)\Big\|_{H^s} &\lesssim TR^{k}A^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A)\sum_{j=1}^\infty T^jR^{j(k-1)}A^{jd(k-1)}\notag\\
    &\lesssim  TR^{k}A^{k-1}f_{s,d}(A).\label{Illposedness:Xi_power_upper:final}
\end{align}
Now, tying these estimates together, we use the reverse triangle inequality on the power series expansion for our solution $u(T)$ who's convergence is guaranteed by Lemma \ref{Power-series-convergence}. Then, we make use of our estimates \eqref{Illposedness:Xi_0:lower_final}, \eqref{proof of prop: Xi_1} and \eqref{Illposedness:Xi_power_upper:final} and \eqref{est: BBM Xi_1 diff} ,
\begin{align*}
    &\left\|u(T)\right\|_{H^s}\\
     &\hphantom{X}\geq \left\|\Xi_1(\psi_n)(T)\right\|_{H^s} - \left\|\Xi_0(u_{0,n})\right\|_{H^s} - \left\|\Xi_1(u_{0,n}(T)) - \Xi_1(\psi_n)(T)\right\|_{H^s}- \Big\|\sum_{j=2}^\infty \Xi_j(u_{0,n}(T))\Big\|_{H^s}\\
    &\hphantom{X}\gtrsim TR^kA^{d(k-1)}f_s(A) - 1+RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s - T\left\|u_0\right\|_{L^2}R^{k-1}A^{d(k-1)}  \\
    &\hphantom{XXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXX}- TR^kA^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A)R^{k-1}A^{d(k-1)}.
\end{align*}
With (i) we get, $ RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s \ll \tfrac{1}{n}$. (ii) and (v) tell us that $T\left\|u_0\right\|_{L^2}R^{k-1}A^{d(k-1)} \ll 1$. Furthermore, (ii) with (iii) yields
\begin{align*}
    TR^kA^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A)R^{k-1}TA^{d(k-1)} \ll TR^kA^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A).
\end{align*}
Thus,
\begin{align*}
    1+RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s + T\left\|u_0\right\|_{L^2}R^{k-1}A^{d(k-1)}  + TR^kA^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A)R^{k-1}A^{d(k-1)} \ll TR^kA^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A).
\end{align*}
Therefore, by property (iv),
\begin{align*}
    \left\|u(T)\right\|_{H^s} &\gtrsim  TR^k A^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A) \gg n.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
To prove Proposition \ref{Thm:quant_norm_inflation}, it remains to verify each of the conditions in Proposition \ref{THM:Norm_inf_to_prop} by choosing an $A$, $R$ and $T$ in terms of $N$. We make the following choices,
\begin{equation}
    R = N^{-\tfrac{s}{2}}, \quad T = N^{(k-\tfrac{1}{2})\tfrac{s}{2}}, \quad \text{and} \quad A = 10\notag.
\end{equation}
We now verify the required conditions. For (i) we get,
\begin{align*}
    RA^{\tfrac{d}{2}}N^s \sim RN^s = N^{-\tfrac{s}{2}}N^s = N^{\tfrac{s}{2}} \ll \tfrac{1}{n}.
\end{align*}
For (ii),
\begin{align*}
    R^{k-1}TA^{d(k-1)} \sim R^{k-1}T = N^{-(k-\tfrac{1}{2})\tfrac{s}{2}}N^{(k-1)\tfrac{s}{2}} = N^{\tfrac{s}{4}} \ll 1.
\end{align*}
For (iii),
\begin{align*}
    R^{k}A^{d(k-1)}f_{s,d}(A) \sim R^{k}A^{d(k-1)} = N^{k\tfrac{s}{2}-\tfrac{s}{4} - k\tfrac{s}{2}} = N^{-\tfrac{s}{4}} \gg n.
\end{align*}
Here, (v) and (iv) are satisfied trivially given that $R\gg 1$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Pick $R,T$ and $A$ as in the proof of Proposition \ref{Thm:quant_norm_inflation}. First, we make the key observation that for $A \lesssim 1$ and some $f\in H^s\cap H^\sigma$, 
    \begin{align}
        \left\|\langle\xi\rangle^s \mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d}}(\xi)\right\|_{\ell^2_\xi} \sim_{s,\sigma} \left\|\langle\xi\rangle^{\sigma} \mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d}}(\xi)\right\|_{\ell^2_\xi}\notag.
    \end{align}
    Using this we draw the following bounds,
    \begin{align}
        \left\|u_n(T)\right\|_{H^\sigma} &\geq \left\|\Pi_{Q_{A,d}} u_n(T)\right\|_{H^\sigma}\notag\\
        &\geq C_{s,\sigma}\big\|\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{A,d}}(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle^s\widehat{u_n(T)}(\xi)\big\|_{\ell^2_\xi}= C_{s,\sigma}\left\| \Pi_{Q_{A,d}} u_n(T) \right\|_{H^s}\notag.
    \end{align}
    As in Proposition \ref{THM:Norm_inf_to_prop} we use the reverse triangle inequality to draw a lower bound in terms of the dominating Picard iterate,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|u_n(T)\right\|_{H^\sigma} &\geq C_{s,\sigma}\bigg(\left\|\Pi_{Q_{A,d}}\Xi_1(\psi_n)(T)\right\|_{H^s} - \left\|\Pi_{Q_{A,d}}\Xi_0(u_{0,n})\right\|_{H^s} \bigg. \\
         &\hphantom{XXXXXX}\bigg.- \Big\|\Pi_{Q_{A,d}}\left(\Xi_1(u_{0,n}(T)) - \Xi_1(\psi_n)(T)\right)\Big\|_{H^s} - \Big\|\Pi_{Q_{A,d}}\sum_{j=2}^\infty \Xi_j(u_{0,n}(T))\Big\|_{H^s}\bigg).
    \end{align*}
    Using Lemma \ref{Lem: LOWERBD}, the fact that $\Pi_{Q_{a,d}}$ is a bounded operator on $H^s$ and Proposition \ref{Thm:quant_norm_inflation} we yield,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|u_n(T)\right\|_{H^\sigma} \gg n
    \end{align*}
    thereby proving Theorem \ref{InfiniteLossofRegs}.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{Def:F_space}
    Let $s\in \mathbb{R}$, $0< q\leq\infty$, $0<p<\infty$ and $m:\mathcal{M} \to [0,\infty)$ be a measurable function. We define the \emph{weighted Triebel-Lizorkin spaces} by,
    \begin{align*}
        F^s_{p,q}(\mathcal{M},w) = \left\{f\in D'(\mathcal{M}): \left\|f\right\|_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathcal{M},w)}<\infty\right\}
    \end{align*}
    where $D'(\mathbb{R}) = \mathscr{S}'(\mathbb{R})$, $D'(\mathbb{T}) = (C^\infty(\mathbb{T}))'$ and\footnote{This definition depends on the choice of the projectors $\mathbf{P}_N$. We will soon assume that $m$ belongs to a space $W$ where the defined norms are equivalent under such a choice. See \cite[Sections 5.1.1, 5.1.2]{triebel1983} for an explaination.}
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|f\right\|_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathcal{M},w)} \coloneqq \Big\|\Big(\sum_{N \text{ dyadic}} N^{qs}|\mathbf{P}_N f|^q\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\Big\|_{L^p(\mathcal{M},w)}.
    \end{align*}
    We write $F^s_{p,q}(\mathcal{M}) = F^s_{p,q}(\mathcal{M},\mathbbm{1}_\mathbb{T})$ for the unweighted Triebel-Lizorkin spaces. We also define the Hardy spaces $h^p(\mathcal{M}) \coloneqq F_{p,2}^0(\mathcal{M})$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{Frac_F_rule}
    Let $0<p<1$, $0<p_1,p_1<\infty$, $s> \min(p,q,1)^{-1}-1$ and $0<q\leq \infty$ such that $\frac{1}{p} = \frac{1}{p_1}+\frac{1}{p_2}$. Then for $u,v \in (C^\infty(\mathbb{T}))'$ there is the estimate,
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|uv\right\|_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{T})}\lesssim \left\|u\right\|_{F_{p_1,q}^s(\mathbb{T})}\left\|v\right\|_{h^{p_2}(\mathbb{T})} + \left\|u\right\|_{h^{p_1}(\mathbb{T})}\left\|v\right\|_{F^s_{p_2,q}(\mathbb{T})}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\cite[Theorem 2.5]{naibo2019}\label{Thm_naibo}
    Let $0<p,p_1,p_2<\infty$, $0<q\leq \infty$ and $s> \min(p,q,1)^{-1}-1$ such that $\frac{1}{p} = \frac{1}{p_1}+ \frac{1}{p_2}$ Then it holds for $u,v\in \mathscr{S}'(\mathbb{R})$
    \begin{align*}
         \left\|uv\right\|_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim \left\|u\right\|_{F_{p_1,q}^s(\mathbb{R})}\left\|v\right\|_{h^{p_2}(\mathbb{R})} + \left\|u\right\|_{h^{p_1}(\mathbb{R})}\left\|v\right\|_{F^s_{p_2,q}(\mathbb{R})}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\cite[Theorem 9.2.1]{triebel1992}\label{FTFRident_lem}
    Let $s\in \mathbb{R}$, $0\leq q <\infty$ and $0<p<\infty$ and $w(x) \coloneqq (1+|x|)^{-\kappa}$ with $\kappa >0$ such that $\kappa p >1$. Then, 
    \begin{align*}
        F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{T}) = F^s_{p,q,\pi}(\mathbb{R},w)
    \end{align*}
    where $F^s_{p,q,\pi}(\mathbb{R},w)$ is the space of periodic distributions on $\mathbb{R}$ restricted by the $F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R},w)$ norm.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def_admissible_w}
    The class $W$ of admissible weight functions is the collection of all possible $C^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ functions $w:\mathbb{R}\to [0,\infty)$ satifying the following properties:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item[(i)] For all $\alpha \in \mathbb{N}$, there exists $C_\alpha >0$ such that for all $x\in \mathbb{R}$,
        \begin{align*}
            |\partial_x^\alpha w(x)| \leq C_\alpha w(x).
        \end{align*}
        \item[(ii)] There exists $C>0$ and $\gamma \geq 0$ such that for all $x,y\in \mathbb{R}$,
        \begin{align*}
            0<w(x) \leq Cw(y)(1+|x-y|^2)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}.
        \end{align*}
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\cite[Proposition 6.13]{triebel2006}\label{A:local_prop}
    Let $s\in \mathbb{R}$, $0<p<\infty$, $0<q\leq \infty$, $w\in W$ and $\psi\in C^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ be a non-negative, compactly supported function such that for all $x\in \mathbb{R}$
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{m\in \mathbb{Z}} \psi_m(x) \sim 1 \quad \psi_m(x) \coloneqq \psi(x-2\pi m) .
    \end{align*}
    Then, the right hand side of the equivalence
    \begin{align*}
        \left\|f\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R},w)}^p \sim \sum_{m \in \mathbb{Z}}w^p(m)\left\|\psi_m f\right\|_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{R})}^p
    \end{align*}
    is finite.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{A:Lem:T_R_loc}
        Let $0<p<\infty$, $0< q\leq \infty$ and $s\in \mathbb{R}$. If $\psi\in C^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ such that $\psi \equiv 1$ on $[0,2\pi]$ and $\operatorname{supp} \psi  = [-0.1,2\pi +0.1]$. Then
        \begin{align*}
            \left\|f\right\|^p_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{T})} \sim \left\|\psi f\right\|^p_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{R})}
        \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Using Lemma \ref{FTFRident_lem} and \eqref{norm_equiv_smooth_weight} we get that
        \begin{align*}
            \left\|f\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{T})}\sim \left\|f\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R} ,w)}.
        \end{align*}
        Now using Proposition \ref{A:local_prop} and the fact that $\tilde{w}\in W$ we see that,
        \begin{align}
            \left\|f\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{T})}^p \sim \sum_{m\in \mathbb{Z}}(1+x^2)^{-\frac{\kappa p}{2}}\left\|\psi_mf\right\|_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{R})}^p\label{A:lem_loc:period}
        \end{align}
        where $\psi_m(x) = \psi(x-2\pi m)$. Noting that $f$ is a periodic distribution, it can be seen by the commutativity of Fourier multipliers with translation that
        \begin{align*}
            \left\|\psi_mf \right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R})}^p \sim  \left\|\psi f\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R})}^p.
        \end{align*}
        Therefore, returning to \eqref{A:lem_loc:period}
        \begin{align*}
            \left\|f\right\|^p_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{T})}\sim \left\|\psi f\right\|_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{R})}^p \sum_{m\in \mathbb{Z}}(1+x^2)^{-\frac{\kappa p}{2}} \sim \left\|\psi f\right\|_{F_{p,q}^s(\mathbb{R})}^p
        \end{align*}
        where the sum converges as $\kappa p >1$.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Let $\psi\in C^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ such that $\psi \equiv 1$ on $[0,2\pi]$ and $\operatorname{supp} \psi =[-0.1,2\pi +0.1]$. By Lemma \ref{A:Lem:T_R_loc} we have that
            \begin{align}
                \left\|uv\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{T})} \sim \left\|\psi uv\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R})}.\label{A:main_proof:loc_step}
            \end{align}
            Now defining $\phi \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ with $\phi \equiv 1$ on $[0,2\pi]$ and $\operatorname{supp} \phi = [-0.2,2\pi + 0.2]$. Therefore,
            \begin{align*}
                \left\|\psi uv\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R})} = \left\|(\psi u)(\phi v)\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R})}.
            \end{align*}
            Returning this to \eqref{A:main_proof:loc_step} and applying Theorem \ref{Thm_naibo} we see that,
            \begin{align}
                \left\|uv\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{T})} &\lesssim \left\|\psi u\right\|_{F^s_{p,q}(\mathbb{R})}\left\|\phi v\right\|_{H^{p_2}} + \left\|\psi u\right\|_{H^{p_1}}\left\|\phi v\right\|_{F^s_{p_2,q}(\mathbb{R})}\label{A:main_proof:pre_transference}.
            \end{align}
           Now using Lemma \ref{A:Lem:T_R_loc} 
            \begin{align*}
                \left\|\psi u\right\|_{F^s_{p_1,q}(\mathbb{R})}\sim \left\|u\right\|_{F^s_{p_1,q}(\mathbb{T})}, \quad \left\|\phi v\right\|_{F^s_{p_2,q}(\mathbb{R})} \sim \left\|v\right\|_{F^s_{p_2,q}(\mathbb{T})}
            \end{align*}
            with the same holding for the other terms. Returning to \eqref{A:main_proof:pre_transference} with this, we yield the desired estimate.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
